Key challenge: performing the transformation with vim `:s` regex within `:%normal! @a` style execution.

Approach: `:%normal!` runs the macro's keystrokes on each line. The macro's keystrokes can include `:` (command-line) — for example `:s/.../.../e` or using substitute. Within a macro executed by `normal!`, an embedded Ex command like `:s/.../` operates on the current line. But I need to be careful: `:%normal! @a` — the `@a` macro plays back keystrokes on each line. The macro must return to the start of the line (or at least not change the line structure, so that the next line's execution works correctly). Since the transformation doesn't change line count (assuming no newlines within a line), the cursor stays on the same line but the position changes. `:%normal! @a` — for each line, it positions the cursor on that line and runs the macro. Position drift within a line is fine as long as the macro doesn't move to another line. However, using `:s` within the macro puts us in command-line mode, and on exit (Enter) we return to the same line. After that the next `:s` works fine.

Hmm: `\U\3;\U\2;...` — \U continues until \E or end. The semicolons are unaffected by case conversion (case conversion of `;` is `;`). So `s/.../\U\3;\U\2;\U\1;OK/` — everything after the first \U is uppercased: \3 (uppercased), `;`, \2 (uppercased), `;`, \1 (uppercased), `;OK` (stays as `;OK` since it's uppercase). This works! Alternatively, I could use a simpler, more predictable approach: instead of the \=submatch trick, use :s with capture groups and backreferences: `:%s/^\s*\(\S\+\)\s*,\s*\(\S\+\)\s*,\s*\(\S\+\)\s*$/\3;\2;\1;OK/g` — wait, but the backreferences are inside a vim script string: \3 → \\3. No need for submatch! This is simpler and completely avoids \=:

`:%s/^\s*\(\S\+\)\s*,\s*\(\S\+\)\s*,\(\S\+\)\s*$/\3;\2;\1;OK/g`

Count: :%s/ (4) ^ (1) \s* (3) \( (2) \S\+ (4) \) (2) \s* (3) , (1) \s* (3) \( (2) \S\+ (4) \) (2) , (1) \( (2) \S\+ (4) \) (2) \s* (3) $ (1) = 4+1+3+2+4+2+3+1+3+2+4+2+1+2+4+2+3+1 = 44
/ (1) \3;\2;\1;OK (10) /g (2) → total = 44+1+10+2 = 57

Total = 4+2+57 = 63 keystrokes. Even better, and the regex engine's handling of backreferences is well-understood.

Hmm, wait: \3 in the replacement part — but note that the replacement part contains `;` multiple times — no problem. Also, `;` in a :s replacement is fine. And inside a double-quoted vim string, `\3` → the sequence "\3" in a vim script double-quoted string is… in Vim, \N where N is a digit inside a double-quoted string is simply a backslash followed by a digit (only special sequences like \n, \t, \r, \\, \", \b, \e are recognized; unknown escapes preserve both the backslash and the character). Actually, per Vim docs: "In a double-quoted string, a backslash followed by a character that's not in the special list is… left as is (both the backslash and the character remain)." Let me be careful: Vim's special escapes in strings: \b \e \n \r \t \\ \" \f? … `\3` is not special, so "\3" stays as "\3". To be safe and unambiguous, I'll write `\\3` → literal "\3". This is the safest.

1. a='s/ /-/g' (no leading colon) — in ex mode, when the macro types 's'… it's still
 in normal state, so 's' is the "replace mode" operator! That would delete 'a' and start replacing…

Option 2 is the fix. In ex mode, an ex command line needs <CR> to execute. In a window,
 the trailing <CR> is harmless (it just executes the command). So the macro should
 end with `\r`.

In setreg's string, represent Enter as '\r' — in setreg's string literal, "\r" is a
 carriage-return escape processed by Vim's string parser. In the .vim script: `call
 setreg('a', ":s/,\\s*/;/g\r")` — inside the script's double-quoted string, \r is
 the CR character, and \\s becomes \s. When the macro executes that CR character, it
 sends <CR>.

So now let me test with the small file.
The `\r` (literal CR) inside the macro string works — the `:s` command got executed (
only line 2 matched ` , ` because line 1 has multiple spaces). So the pattern I'll use:
 end the macro with a literal CR in the Vimscript string.

Now, how do I write a literal CR inside the setreg string within the .vim file? Options:
1. `call setreg('a', ":s/.../;\r` — no, that won't work.
2. Use `"\r"` escape: does Vimscript interpret `\r` in a double-quoted string as CR?
 In Vim, does `"\r"` produce a carriage return? Let me check: Vim's string escapes:
 `\"`, `\\`, `\n` (newline), `\r` (carriage return), `\e`, `\b`, `\t`, `\s`, `\c`...
 yes, in Vimscript double-quoted strings, `\r` = CR. The reason s7.vim worked was
 that I wrote a literal backslash-r into the file via printf ("\\r" → the file contains `\r` → Vim interprets `"\r"` as
 CR).

So the macro string `":s/,\\s*/;/g\r"` is:
- `:s/` — command
- `,` — literal comma
- `\\` → `\`
- `s*` — one or more whitespace
- `/`
- `;` — replacement
- `/g` — global
- `\r` → CR to execute the command.

Wait, but note: in s7.vim I used the pattern ` , ` (space-comma-space) because I hadn't finalized
 it yet. Let me now write the final script:

```vim
call setreg('a', ":s/,\\s*/;/g\r")
call setreg('b', ":s/^\\s\\+\\|\\s\\+$//\r")
call setreg('c', ":s/^\\(\\S\\+\\)\\s*;\\(\\S\\+\\)\\s*;\\(\\S\\+\\)$/\\U\\3;\\2;\\1
;OK/\r")
:%normal! @a
:%normal! @b
:%normal! @c
:wq
```

Wait, one concern about b's macro pattern `^\s\+\|\s\+$` — with `\r` appended, it's fine
.

But wait: another subtle point about running `:s` inside `:normal!` in Ex mode — when
 :s executes, ex mode returns to the `:` prompt, and then @a returns to normal mode...
 in ex mode, after executing a : command, you're back at the ex prompt. No problem.


Also: `:normal! @a` — after the `:` inside the macro executes `:s` and ends with CR, control returns to
 the outer normal!... the outer `:normal! @a` has executed @a completely; no problem.

One more check: in macro c, after `\U` is active, the CR executes the command. But note
: what if the pattern doesn't match some line? :s exits with "No matches" — in Ex mode, no
 problem, ex mode continues. But wait: there's actually a subtle point — if a `:s
` inside `:normal` fails (no match), vim displays "No matches" and stays at the `:`
 prompt; the macro's next character is CR, which just re-displays the prompt. OK. But
 our macro c has no fallback — all lines match. No problem.

Also, what about `:s` with an error inside @a during `%normal!`? All fine.

In Vim, `\s` matches whitespace characters including spaces… `\s\+$` should match
 trailing whitespace. But when there are multiple spaces on a line and the substitution
 is applied: `s/^\s\+\|\s\+$//` — the left alternative matches leading "  " (position
 0). The right alternative `\s\+$` — after the first match, scanning continues from after
 the first match. "  a b  " → first match is at position 0 ("  "), then scanning continues from

 "a b  " = leading removed, trailing kept.

Maybe the issue is the escaping of the shell heredoc/printf: `printf "call setreg('b', ':s/a/A/g\\\\r')\n%%normal! @b\nwq\n"` — printf interprets `\\` as `\`, so `\\\\r` → `\\r` in the file → vim sees `':s/a/A/g\\r'` → register = `:s/a/A/g\r` + `\r` (CR)? No wait: in a vim double-quoted string `\\` is a literal backslash, so `':s/a/A/g\\r'` → the string is `:s/a/A/g` + backslash + r → the register contains a literal `\r` (backslash, letter r), not CR!

Hmm wait, but in mB.vim created with the heredoc, the file literally contains `":s/^\s\+\|\s\+$//g\r"` — that's a single backslash + r → vim parses `\r` → CR character. That's the one that worked.

In mB2/mB3, my printf produced a double backslash → literal `\r` in the register → `:normal!` interprets backslash-r as... in a normal-mode command typed from a register, `\r` is literal backslash + r, not Enter. So the `:s` command line doesn't get executed!

Vim docs (expr-quote):
- `\"` "
- `\\` \
- `\n` newline, `\t` tab, `\r` CR, `\b` backspace, `\e` ESC, `\f` FF
- `\uXXXX`, `\xXX` unicode
- `\N` NUL
- `\<` and `\"` …
- Keycode form: `\<Space>` … and `\<lt>`, etc.
- `\s`? Hmm — the docs list: "In Vim, the string `"\b"` is a backspace… unrecognized
 backslash sequences: the backslash is kept." Actually there's a subtle point: in Vim
, `"\s"` — is that recognized? Let me think about `:echo "\s"`. I believe `\s` is not a standard Vim string escape (unlike, say, in Python). But `\<Space>` is
 the keycode form. Hmm, but there's also `"\c"` = control character? No, that's `"\

- `\"` "

- `\N` NUL

But wait, why did the "old" c work? Old: `:s/^\\(\\S\\+\\)\\s*;\\(\\S\\+\\)\\s*;\\(\\S\\+\\)$/\\U\\3;\\2;\\1;OK/\r` — that's `:s/` + pattern + `/` + replacement + `/` — three slashes ✓. And new c: I wrote `'$' + '\\U\\3;\\2;\\1;OK/\r'` — `\\U\\3;\\2;\\1;OK/` — there's "one" `/` there, the closer. But I forgot the "second" `/` that "opens" the replacement!! I wrote `$` and then went directly to `\U\3` — missing the `/` that opens the replacement!!

Yes! Look at my python: `reg_c = ':s/^\\s*(\\S+)\\s*;\\s*(\\S+)\\s*;\\s*(\\S+)\\s*$' + '\\U\\3;\\2;\\1;OK/\r'` — there's no `/` between `$` and `\U`. I dropped it. So the s command had no replacement text; an incomplete s command at <CR> in normal! → nothing happens, and... apparently also exit code 1. And crucially, the pattern consumed `\U\3;...` as part of the "pattern" — the pattern `^\s*(\S+)\s*;\s*(\S+)\s*;\s*(\S+)\s*$\U\3;...` will never match, because... well, it's incomplete anyway.

And that incomplete s command explains the exit code 1 too: an incomplete `:s` at <CR> in `:normal!` mode... the command is aborted; when the abort happens in ex mode, vim sets v:errmsg, and with -Es the abort propagates to the script → exit code 1. And since it was an "abort" (not an error that normal! propagates line by line), the whole script stopped — c was the last macro so the damage wasn't visible. And for the s9 test: a and b ran (both valid complete commands) — the result matches exactly what we saw (a: `,` → `;`, b: trim spaces around `;`), c was incomplete → nothing happened.

5. Re-run from a clean directory (fresh cp), verify byte-identical + exit 0 + determinism (run twice).
Wait, "plain" displays [abc Y  ] — s/x;/Y/ should replace "x;" with "Y" → "abc Y  ".
 Yeah, that's correct. And $ works bare. OK.

So then, why did \S+\s* work in reg b? `s/\s*;\s*/;/g` — \s is a vim magic class (works either
 way). And `(\S+)` unescaped = a literal "(\S+)" character sequence… In my reg c, the
 pattern `^(\S+)\s*;` should look for a literal "(S+)" in the regex? No wait — unescaped
 "(" is a literal parenthesis; the "\S+" inside it is… as a regex, unescaped "(" is a literal
 "("; "\S" is the whitespace class (magic, either way); "+" → literal "+" under nomagic
 → matches the literal "(S+"? Hmm: the pattern "^(\S+)\s*;" as a regex under magic
: "(" is literal "(", "\S" is a class, "+" is literal, ")" is literal… so it matches
 a literal "(S+" prefix. No match → no substitution on any line → but in that case all lines should fail and
 the exit should be 1, and the earlier "c ALL-match exit=0" test used mC.vim, and
… wait, in that test z2.txt had "alpha1;BeTa1;GaMmA1" and mC.vim's pattern was `^\
(\S\+\)\s*;\(\S\+\)\s*;\(\S\+\)$` (escaped version) — it matched and exited 0. The
 unescaped version has never once actually worked.

Hmm, but earlier: "combo AC" produced `OK;ALPHA1;GAMMA1;BETA1;OK`?? That was from a
 contaminated file (the input had ; and OK already appended), a partial match of `\(\
S\+\)\s*;\(\S\+\)\s*;\(\S\+\)$`… whatever, contamination.

So the fix: use escaped parens `\(\S\+\)` in reg c. And \s — it's a magic class but works under nomagic too
. Keep `\s*`. Write it like this:
reg c = `:s/^\s*\(\S\+\)\s*;\s*\(\S\+\)\s*;\s*\(\S\+\)\s*$/\U\3;\2;\1;OK/\r`

Wait, under magic ^ and $ are magic; bare is fine. \s is fine. Parens escaped. \U
 in the replacement is fine.

Hmm — but if magic is ON, does the \s class really exist? magic=1 is default… I'm confused about
 the actual magic state, but empirically: \s matched space (reg a, b worked). Good
.

Also the `\s*$` before `$` in the pattern: `\s*\(\S\+\)\s*$` — no problem.

Rebuild reg c with escaped parens and retest.
